Correspondence of Contradictions in the Constructive Connexive Calculus C
Authors: Satoru Niki
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
The calculus C was introduced by H. Wansing as a constructive logic with strong negation. In addition, C validates the theses of connexive logic that are attributed to Aristotle and Boethius. A further remarkable property of C is that it is a non-trivial but negation inconsistent system: it has a formula and its negation as theorems. From a bilateralist-minded perspective, such a contradiction can be seen as the existence of both a verification and a falsification of one and the same formula. Relatedly, it has been noted by Wansing that there seems to be a kind of correspondence between these two types of derivations when it comes to a proof of contradiction. Following this observation, we attempt in this paper to introduce a precise notion for such a correspondence. We thence establish that this correspondence obtains in propositional and first-order versions of C, via formulations of suitable sequent and tableau calculi.

Introduction

The calculus C, introduced by H. Wansing [22], is an expansion of positive intuitionistic logic with a negation which exhibits connexive features: it validates classically invalid schemata known as Aristotle’s theses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathord {\sim }(\mathord {\sim }A\rightarrow A)$$\end{document}∼(∼A→A) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathord {\sim }(A\rightarrow \mathord {\sim }A)$$\end{document}∼(A→∼A), as well as Boethius’ theses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow \mathord {\sim }(A\rightarrow \mathord {\sim }B)$$\end{document}(A→B)→∼(A→∼B) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(A\rightarrow \mathord {\sim }B)\rightarrow \mathord {\sim }(A\rightarrow B)$$\end{document}(A→∼B)→∼(A→B). (see [12, 24, 27] for details.) It can also be seen as a variant of A. Almukdad and D. Nelson’s constructive paraconsistent logic N4 [1]. The negation of C can for this reason be categorised as a constructible falsity or a strong negation [14]. Proof-theoretically1, this means that C can be seen as a system which treats the notions of verification and falsification on a par, so that a sequent can be formulated in the form2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}A$$\end{document}Γ:Δ⇒∗A, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$*\in \{+,-\}$$\end{document}∗∈{+,-} stands for these notions, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta $$\end{document}Δ are premises on falsified/verified formulas. The negation then serves as a bridge between the two notions, and a provable contradiction can be rephrased as a simultaneous verification and falsification of the same formula.

Interesting aspects of C are not limited to connexivity. Another remarkable property of C is that it is a (non-trivial) negation inconsistent system, in the sense that it has a contradictory pair of a formula and its negation as theorems. The key for the negation inconsistency lies in the treatment of negated implication, since it is the only difference from the negation consistent N4. If we employ the aforementioned perspective of sequents, then it is possible (as we shall see) to point out a parallel treatment of the verification and falsification of an implication. Heinrich Wansing pointed out to the author (in a private communication) that a generalisation of such a correspondence can be found in derivations of a contradiction: His example concerns the formula \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathord {\sim }A\rightarrow \mathord {\sim }(A\rightarrow \mathord {\sim }A)$$\end{document}∼A→∼(A→∼A). This formula can be both refuted and verified by the next pair of derivations:

where the fourth and the last lines exhibit parallel rules for introducing implication, and the second, third and fifth lines exhibit parallel rules for introducing negation.

The question we ask in this paper is to what extent this example can be generalised. The aim therefore is to spell out the correspondence between verification and falsification implicit in the above example, and to establish that such a correspondence is obtainable for any given instance of provable contradictions in C. Achieving this aim leads to a better understanding of the negation inconsistency of C, as it shows a necessary structural relationship between the notions of verification and falsification for provable contradictions. Furthermore, due to the dependence of the enquiry on specific proof systems, a characterisation may also give an insight into the question of which proof system is conceptually to be preferred.

This paper is structured as follows: in Section 2, we shall introduce the propositional sequent calculus used in the above example, and show properties that are necessary for our investigation. In addition, we recall the Kripke semantics for C introduced in [22] which will be used in some places. Section 3 first defines the notions of correspondence. Then as an initial step we shall investigate the implication-negation fragment of C, and show how a pair of derivations satisfying the correspondence can be constructed from a given instance of provable contradictions. This is followed by an analysis in terms of propositional variables that offers a necessary condition all derivations of a provable contradiction must satisfy. In Section 4, we shall attempt to generalise the results of the previous section in the full propositional calculus, the initial outcome of which is a correspondence under a restriction on formulas. We will then suggest a way to remove this restriction by transforming the sequent calculus to a Mints-style [13] tableau calculus. Finally, Section 5 and 6 develop the analysis for the first-order version of C by slightly altering the definitions of correspondence.

Preliminaries for the propositional case

In this section, we shall introduce target propositional logics of our enquiry via proof systems and semantics.

In each case, a derivation of a formula A from a set of formulas \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ is a finite sequence of formulas \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{1},\ldots ,B_{n}\equiv A$$\end{document}B1,…,Bn≡A, where each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{i}$$\end{document}Bi is either an instance of an axiom schema, an element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ, or obtained from preceding formulas by (MP).

For our enquiry, we shall mainly concern ourselves with another type of proof system, namely sequent calculus. Following the presentation of Wansing’s example, the system we shall use is a simple modification of bilateral3 sequent calculi Sn4 and Dn4 introduced by N. Kamide and Wansing in [9, 10] for N4. More specifically, a sequent will be of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}A$$\end{document}Γ:Δ⇒∗A, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta $$\end{document}Δ are finite multisets of formulas called negative/positive antecedent, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$*\in \{+,-\}$$\end{document}∗∈{+,-} is the sign of the consequent formula. This framework is suitable for our purpose in simplifying definitions and making explicit the role of the polarity that is kept implicit in the Hilbert-style calculi.

When it comes to structural rules, we shall use a G3-type formulation [15, 21] which absorbs them. Some other rules are defined like in GK-type systems [21], in that their principal formula (the displayed formula in the conclusion) is duplicated in the premises.4

The sequent calculus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{bGKC}$$\end{document}bGKC is defined by the following set of rules:

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$*\in \{+,-\}$$\end{document}∗∈{+,-} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \{1,2\}$$\end{document}i∈{1,2}. The implication-negation system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{bGKC}_{\rightarrow }$$\end{document}bGKC→ is then defined in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\rightarrow }$$\end{document}L→ by removing the rules for conjunction and disjunction. A derivation of a sequent is a finite tree which has the sequent as the root, and whose leaves (initial sequents) are instances of (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Ax}^-$$\end{document}Ax-) or (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Ax}^+$$\end{document}Ax+), and the sequent in each non-leaf node is obtained by one of the rules from sequent(s) of the preceding nodes.

Given a derivation in a sequent calculus, its depth is the number of non-leaf nodes in the longest branch of the tree (it can be defined for a derivation in a Hilbert-style system by considering it as a tree).

Both (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Ax}^-$$\end{document}Ax-) and (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Ax}^+$$\end{document}Ax+) can be generalised into all formulas.

Proposition 1

The following statements hold. Proof

By induction on the complexity of A. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Cut-elimination

One desirable property for a sequent calculus is cut-elimination. For bGKC, this can be shown in a standard manner (see e.g. [15, 21]), with a small difference that we have two cut rules, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Rightarrow ^{+}$$\end{document}⇒+ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Rightarrow ^{-}$$\end{document}⇒- (cf. the cases for Sn4 and Dn4 [9, 10]).

Let us use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}$$\end{document}⊢k to denote the derivability of a sequent with depth at most k. We shall say a rule is depth-preserving admissible if the existence of derivations of its premises implies the existence of a derivation of its conclusion with depth at most the maximum depth of the derivations of the premises. When there is no restriction about the depth, we shall say a rule is admissible.

We begin with observing the depth-preserving admissibility of weakening rules.

Lemma 2

The next rules are depth-preserving admissible in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }$$\end{document}bGKC→ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{bGKC}$$\end{document}bGKC.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C}{A,\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C} {\textit{(}LW^-\textit{)}} \frac{\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C}{\Gamma :\Delta ,A\Rightarrow ^{*}C} {\textit{(}LW^+\textit{)}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Γ:Δ⇒∗CA,Γ:Δ⇒∗C(LW-)Γ:Δ⇒∗CΓ:Δ,A⇒∗C(LW+)

Proof

By induction on the depth of derivations. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The weakening rules can be used to show that most5 of the rules in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }$$\end{document}bGKC→ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{bGKC}$$\end{document}bGKC are (depth-preserving) invertible, i.e. the existence of a derivation of the conclusion guarantees the existence of a derivation of the premise without an increase in depth.

Specifically, we have the next lemma.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k} \Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{+}A_{1}\wedge A_{2}$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ⇒+A1∧A2 then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{+}A_{i}$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ⇒+Ai.

(xii)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k} A\vee B,\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kA∨B,Γ:Δ⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}A,B,\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kA,B,Γ:Δ⇒∗C.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k} A\vee B,\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kA∨B,Γ:Δ⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}A,B,\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kA,B,Γ:Δ⇒∗C.

(xiii)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{-}A_{1}\vee A_{2}$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ⇒-A1∨A2 then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{-}A_{i}$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ⇒-Ai.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{-}A_{1}\vee A_{2}$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ⇒-A1∨A2 then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{-}A_{i}$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ⇒-Ai.

(xiv)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta ,A_{1}\vee A_{2}\Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,A1∨A2⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta ,A_{1}\vee A_{2},A_{i}\Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,A1∨A2,Ai⇒∗C.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta ,A_{1}\vee A_{2}\Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,A1∨A2⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta ,A_{1}\vee A_{2},A_{i}\Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,A1∨A2,Ai⇒∗C.

Proof

By induction on the depth of derivations, with Lemma 2 for (v)–(ix), (xiv). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

This in turn implies the depth-preserving admissibility of contraction rules.

Lemma 4

The next rules are depth-preserving admissible in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }$$\end{document}bGKC→ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{bGKC}$$\end{document}bGKC.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{A,A,\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C}{A,\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C} {\textit{(}LC^-\textit{)}} \frac{\Gamma :\Delta ,A,A\Rightarrow ^{*}C}{\Gamma :\Delta ,A\Rightarrow ^{*}C} {\textit{(}LC^+\textit{)}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A,A,Γ:Δ⇒∗CA,Γ:Δ⇒∗C(LC-)Γ:Δ,A,A⇒∗CΓ:Δ,A⇒∗C(LC+)

Proof

The cases follow from inductions on the depth of derivations. The cases for (L\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\wedge ^-$$\end{document}∧-) and (L\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vee ^+$$\end{document}∨+) use Lemma 3. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Contraction guarantees more G3-style inversions of some of the left rules.

The following statements hold in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }$$\end{document}bGKC→ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{bGKC}$$\end{document}bGKC. (iii)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k} A_{1}\wedge A_{2},\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kA1∧A2,Γ:Δ⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}A_{i},\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kAi,Γ:Δ⇒∗C.(iv)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta ,A_{1}\vee A_{2}\Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,A1∨A2⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta ,A_{i}\Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,Ai⇒∗C.

(i)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}A\rightarrow B,\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*} C$$\end{document}⊢kA→B,Γ:Δ⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}B,\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*} C$$\end{document}⊢kB,Γ:Δ⇒∗C.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}A\rightarrow B,\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*} C$$\end{document}⊢kA→B,Γ:Δ⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}B,\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*} C$$\end{document}⊢kB,Γ:Δ⇒∗C.

(ii)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta , A\rightarrow B \Rightarrow ^{*} C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,A→B⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta , B \Rightarrow ^{*} C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,B⇒∗C.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta , A\rightarrow B \Rightarrow ^{*} C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,A→B⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta , B \Rightarrow ^{*} C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,B⇒∗C.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k} A_{1}\wedge A_{2},\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kA1∧A2,Γ:Δ⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}A_{i},\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kAi,Γ:Δ⇒∗C.

(iv)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta ,A_{1}\vee A_{2}\Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,A1∨A2⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta ,A_{i}\Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,Ai⇒∗C.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta ,A_{1}\vee A_{2}\Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,A1∨A2⇒∗C then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash _{k}\Gamma :\Delta ,A_{i}\Rightarrow ^{*}C$$\end{document}⊢kΓ:Δ,Ai⇒∗C.

Proof

By induction on the depth of derivations, using Lemma 4. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We are now ready to show the admissibility of cut in bGKC and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }$$\end{document}bGKC→.

Theorem 6

The following rules are admissible in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{bGKC}$$\end{document}bGKC and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }$$\end{document}bGKC→.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{-} A \qquad A,\Gamma ':\Delta '\Rightarrow ^{*}C}{\Gamma ,\Gamma ':\Delta ,\Delta '\Rightarrow ^{*}C} {\textit{(}Cut^-\textit{)}} \frac{\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{+} A \qquad \Gamma ':\Delta ',A\Rightarrow ^{*}C}{\Gamma ,\Gamma ':\Delta ,\Delta '\Rightarrow ^{*}C} {\textit{(}Cut^+\textit{)}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Γ:Δ⇒-AA,Γ′:Δ′⇒∗CΓ,Γ′:Δ,Δ′⇒∗C(Cut-)Γ:Δ⇒+AΓ′:Δ′,A⇒∗CΓ,Γ′:Δ,Δ′⇒∗C(Cut+)

Proof

Let us call the formula represented above by A the cutformula. The statement will be shown by induction on the complexity of the cutformula, with a subinduction on the level of cut (sum of the depths of the subderivations of the premises). The cases for (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Cut}^-$$\end{document}Cut-) and (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Cut}^+$$\end{document}Cut+) are simultaneously treated. Here we look at two cases for (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Cut}^-$$\end{document}Cut-) where the cutformula is principal in both of the premises.

If the cutformula has the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathord {\sim }B$$\end{document}∼B, then we have the next instance: Then we can construct the next derivation:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{+}A \qquad \Gamma ':\Delta ',A\Rightarrow ^{*}C}{\Gamma ,\Gamma ':\Delta ,\Delta '\Rightarrow ^{*}C} {(\text {Cut}^+)} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Γ:Δ⇒+AΓ′:Δ′,A⇒∗CΓ,Γ′:Δ,Δ′⇒∗C(Cut+)where the (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Cut}^+$$\end{document}Cut+) is of less complexity, and so admissible by the I.H..

If the cutformula has the from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B\rightarrow C$$\end{document}B→C, then we have the next instance for (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Cut}^-$$\end{document}Cut-): Then on one hand we can derive: where the upper (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Cut}^-$$\end{document}Cut-) is admissible as it has less level, while the lower (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Cut}^+$$\end{document}Cut+) is admissible as it has less complexity. The derivation is continued as follows, utilising again the admissibility of (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Cut}^-$$\end{document}Cut-) by the I.H. along with that of (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {LC}^-$$\end{document}LC-) and (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {LC}^+$$\end{document}LC+).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

As a consequence of Theorem 6, we can establish the correspondence between the Hilbert-style systems and the sequent calculi. Let us define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^{-}$$\end{document}A- and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^{+}$$\end{document}A+ to be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathord {\sim }A$$\end{document}∼A and A itself, respectively. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma ^{-}$$\end{document}Γ- denotes the multiset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{A^{-}:A\in \Gamma \}$$\end{document}{A-:A∈Γ}. Moreover, we adopt the convention that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigwedge \Gamma \equiv \top $$\end{document}⋀Γ≡⊤ if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma =\emptyset $$\end{document}Γ=∅. Finally, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{1},\ldots , B_{n}$$\end{document}B1,…,Bn are the elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma \rightarrow A$$\end{document}Γ→A stands for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{1}\rightarrow (B_{2}\rightarrow (\ldots \rightarrow (B_{n}\rightarrow A))$$\end{document}B1→(B2→(…→(Bn→A)).

Corollary 7

The following statements hold. (i)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }\vdash \Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}A$$\end{document}bGKC→⊢Γ:Δ⇒∗A if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {C}}_{\rightarrow }\vdash \Gamma ^{-}\rightarrow (\Delta \rightarrow A^{*})$$\end{document}C→⊢Γ-→(Δ→A∗).(ii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{bGKC}\vdash \Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}A$$\end{document}bGKC⊢Γ:Δ⇒∗A if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}\vdash (\bigwedge \Gamma ^{-}\wedge \bigwedge \Delta )\rightarrow A^{*}$$\end{document}C⊢(⋀Γ-∧⋀Δ)→A∗.

(i)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }\vdash \Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}A$$\end{document}bGKC→⊢Γ:Δ⇒∗A if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {C}}_{\rightarrow }\vdash \Gamma ^{-}\rightarrow (\Delta \rightarrow A^{*})$$\end{document}C→⊢Γ-→(Δ→A∗).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }\vdash \Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}A$$\end{document}bGKC→⊢Γ:Δ⇒∗A if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {C}}_{\rightarrow }\vdash \Gamma ^{-}\rightarrow (\Delta \rightarrow A^{*})$$\end{document}C→⊢Γ-→(Δ→A∗).

(ii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{bGKC}\vdash \Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}A$$\end{document}bGKC⊢Γ:Δ⇒∗A if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}\vdash (\bigwedge \Gamma ^{-}\wedge \bigwedge \Delta )\rightarrow A^{*}$$\end{document}C⊢(⋀Γ-∧⋀Δ)→A∗.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{bGKC}\vdash \Gamma :\Delta \Rightarrow ^{*}A$$\end{document}bGKC⊢Γ:Δ⇒∗A if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}\vdash (\bigwedge \Gamma ^{-}\wedge \bigwedge \Delta )\rightarrow A^{*}$$\end{document}C⊢(⋀Γ-∧⋀Δ)→A∗.

Proof

(i) The right-to-left direction follows from showing that the derivability of A in the Hilbert-style systems implies the derivability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ :\ \Rightarrow ^{+}A$$\end{document}:⇒+A in the sequent calculi, by induction on the depth of derivations in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {C}}_{\rightarrow }$$\end{document}C→. The statements then follow as a particular case, with the the help of (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Cut}^-$$\end{document}Cut-) and (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Cut}^+$$\end{document}Cut+). The left-to-right directions follow from induction on the depth of derivations in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }$$\end{document}bGKC→. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Theorem 8

The following statements hold. (i)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }\vdash \ :\ \Rightarrow ^{+}A$$\end{document}bGKC→⊢:⇒+A if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {C}}_{\rightarrow }\vDash A$$\end{document}C→⊨A.(ii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{bGKC}\vdash \ :\ \Rightarrow ^{+}A$$\end{document}bGKC⊢:⇒+A if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}\vDash A$$\end{document}C⊨A.

(i)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }\vdash \ :\ \Rightarrow ^{+}A$$\end{document}bGKC→⊢:⇒+A if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {C}}_{\rightarrow }\vDash A$$\end{document}C→⊨A.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {bGKC}}_{\rightarrow }\vdash \ :\ \Rightarrow ^{+}A$$\end{document}bGKC→⊢:⇒+A if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {C}}_{\rightarrow }\vDash A$$\end{document}C→⊨A.
